Written because \"it is immutable afterwards\" is the right thing to\r\nworry about before the fact, and because the answer changes two of the v3 specs.\r\n\r\n## The question\r\n\r\nAn immutable program cannot be upgraded, so every future version is a **new program id**.\r\nThat is not a limitation to design around; it is the only shape succession has on Solana once\r\nthe authority is gone. The real question is narrower and answerable:\r\n\r\n**How much is lost at the boundary between one version and the next?**\r\n\r\n## What the record shows: Serum\r\n\r\nThe clearest case is Serum. Its program's upgrade keys were held by FTX rather than by the SRM\r\nDAO, and when FTX collapsed the key stopped being a convenience and became a liability\r\novernight �?\" a live key in the hands of an entity in bankruptcy proceedings, capable of\r\nchanging a program the whole Solana DeFi stack was routing through.\r\n\r\nNobody could revoke it, because revoking requires holding it. The ecosystem's only available\r\nremedy was to **fork the program under a new id** �?\" OpenBook, deployed 14 November 2022 �?\" and\r\nre-integrate everything downstream around the new address.\r\n\r\nTwo things follow, and they point in opposite directions from where intuition puts them:\r\n\r\n- **A retained upgrade key is not neutral while unused.** It is a dormant liability that\r\n  activates when its holder does, and its holder's future is not something you control. This\r\n  is the strongest available argument for 2026-09-08 and it is an empirical one.\r\n- **Succession happened anyway, and it cost the state.** OpenBook is a separate program with\r\n  its own markets. Nothing carried across by itself.\r\n\r\nSo: freezing does not create the succession problem. Succession is the permanent condition of\r\nany program anyone will still care about in three years. A\r\nsuccessor can read around a bad field but can never repair one. The player does\r\nnot choose the reference, so the question is structurally close to a coin flip and cannot\r\nbe selected for easiness. Threshold shots (`kind 1` / `kind 2`) let the player pick the\r\nstrike, and \"will BTC be above $1 in five minutes\" is a 99% question anyone can farm. Anyone may reveal a settled shot; the credit lands on\r\n`shot.player`, never on the revealer. A stranger who reveals your shot does you a favour\r\nand gains nothing, which is the same shape as `close_shot`.\r\n\r\n## How another program uses it\r\n\r\n`Calibration` is an ordinary account, and ownership on Solana gates writes, not reads. Any\r\nSolana program �?\" including a future version of this one, deployed under a different id after\r\nthis one is frozen �?\" can read it with a standard deserialize and gate on it �?\" a lending market weighting a liquidation oracle, a DAO\r\nweighting a vote, an agent marketplace pricing a subscription by the seller's track record.\r\nThe read costs nothing and needs no permission from us, which is the property that makes\r\nthis infrastructure rather than a leaderboard.\r\n\r\nShip alongside: a `ratchet-calibration` crate with the account layout and the derivation\r\nhelpers, so integrating is copy-paste rather than archaeology.\r\n\r\n## What this score does not mean\r\n\r\nIt is scoped to direction shots on the feeds this program settles, over the horizons the\r\nplayer chose. It is not a general forecasting rating and must never be sold as one. Someone\r\ncalibrated on five-minute SOL is not thereby calibrated on elections, and the account\r\ncarries the horizon sum precisely so that nobody can pretend otherwise.\r\n\r\n## Order of work\r\n\r\n1.
